Difference: The pads need no SDF and no per-step patch extraction. Newton's hydroelastic path writes c without the effective-mass factor: the bed sink was 7.1–7.4× Drake's until kh was multiplied by m_eff. Reduction cut the arm by 16–25 %. Pads: 1.08 µs per world-step against 1.85 µs.
- Nechyporenko, Abderezaei, Roncone, 'Compliant Sphere Lattice Contact: Distributed Contact Modeling for Sphere-Based Robot Representations', CR2 workshop at ICRA 2026, arXiv 2608.00263 (2026) https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00263: Surface spheres on a robot body tied by anchor springs and graph-Laplacian lateral springs, with saturating presliding friction. The lattice is solved quasi-statically each step by damped Jacobi, hosted in MuJoCo and PBD-R. About 3× the cost of point contact. Tests: point-load decay (ℓ = 4.24 mm), squeeze–lift–hold, and a book pitch of ±0.7° against more than 6° for point contact. Overlap: Sphere-based fingertips that produce a distributed patch and rotational stiffness. Same university as the owner (CU Boulder, HIRO group). Difference: The pads have no lattice state, no lateral coupling and no presliding, and realise a physical E·a/h inside the host's constraint rows. CSLC sets k_c directly, never tests torsion about the normal, and makes no hydroelastic or Drake comparison.
- Sherman, Seth, Delp, 'Simbody: multibody dynamics for biomedical research', Procedia IUTAM 2, 2011 (ElasticFoundationForce in Simbody and OpenSim) (2011) https://simtk.org/api_docs/simtkcore/api_docs21/classSimTK_1_1ElasticFoundationForce.html: A spring at the centre of every triangle-mesh face, f = k·a·x·(1 + c·v), with k derived from an elastic layer's E, ν and thickness. Each spring has its own static/dynamic/viscous friction, which 'cannot represent stiction': a small drift always remains, capped by vt. Overlap: The same discrete Winkler foundation with per-element friction, and the same creep below slip. Difference: An explicit penalty force element on CPU at mesh faces. The pads are rows of MuJoCo's implicit soft-constraint solver with elliptic cones, batched on the GPU, with sphere sites.
- Bei, Fregly, 'Multibody dynamic simulation of knee contact mechanics', Medical Engineering & Physics 26(9), 2004 (2004) https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1680082: Couples an elastic-foundation (Winkler) contact model of meshed articular surfaces with multibody dynamics of the knee. Overlap: Elastic-foundation contact inside a multibody simulator, with stiffness from layer modulus and thickness. Difference: Biomechanics, CPU, frictionless joints; no friction torque or GPU batching.
- Gonthier, McPhee, Lange, Piedboeuf, volumetric contact model (MSD 2004; ASME IDETC 2005, 2007); Gonthier PhD thesis 2007; Boos MASc thesis 2011 (validation) (2004–2011) https://uwspace.uwaterloo.ca/handle/10012/3416: A Winkler foundation in closed form through the overlap volume, its centroid and its inertia tensor. Rolling resistance and spinning-friction torque follow from the pressure distribution, with a dwell-time bristle friction model. Experimental validation for sphere and cylinder on a plane. Overlap: Friction torque about the normal derived from a Winkler pressure distribution: the pads' central physics. Difference: Analytic volumes that assume a flat contact surface. The pads sample arbitrary fingertip geometry with spheres inside MuJoCo, with no flatness assumption.
- Elandt, Drumwright, Sherman, Ruina, 'A pressure field model for fast, robust approximation of net contact force and moment between nominally rigid objects', IROS 2019 (2019) https://arxiv.org/abs/1904.11433: Pressure Field Contact (Drake hydroelastic): a precomputed internal pressure field per body, with the contact surface where the two fields are equal. The authors describe it as a generalisation of an elastic foundation. Overlap: The law the pads sample (p = E·d/R on the sphere tip) and the bed's reference. Difference: A continuous field integrated over the contact surface; the pads sample it at fixed surface sites.
- Castro, Permenter, Han, 'An unconstrained convex formulation of compliant contact', IEEE T-RO 39, 2023; Castro, Han, Masterjohn, 'A theory of irrotational contact fields', arXiv 2312.03908, 2023 (2023) https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.10107: The SAP solver: exact for sticking contact, with an O(δt·‖v_t‖) gliding effect while sliding. The second paper shows that SAP's compliant model and Todorov's regularized model (MuJoCo) both glide, and that the gliding does not vanish as δt → 0. Overlap: The basis of the creep comparison: regularized friction slides below the slip load; Drake creeps 0.2 µm/s on the bed. Difference: No closed form for MuJoCo's per-row creep in terms of sphere count. The overview's (12) gives v = F_t/(2·n·κ·t_c·K).
- MuJoCo documentation (Todorov, Erez, Tassa, IROS 2012; docs 2026): condim 4 and 6, slow slippage, impratio and noslip, refsafe, soft-constraint impedance (2012–2026) https://mujoco.readthedocs.io/en/stable/modeling.html: Torsional and rolling friction with fixed coefficients in units of length. A steady slip velocity under a persistent tangential load is stated as expected; elliptic cones, a large impratio and noslip reduce it. The time constant must be at least 2× the step (refsafe). At dampratio 1 the steady penetration under a constant load is independent of effective mass. Overlap: The solver facts behind overview steps 6–9: rest stiffness, inverse-inertia dependence, creep and the step bound. Difference: No recipe for a per-sphere force stiffness, no creep formula, and no bound for many rows pressing one body.
- Gazebo classic, torsional friction (ODE), Gazebo 7+ (2016) https://classic.gazebosim.org/tutorials?tut=torsional_friction: T = (3π/16)·a·μ·N on a single contact, with a fixed patch_radius or a = √(R·d) from the penetration depth; ODE only. Overlap: Load-scheduled torsion on one contact point: the analogue of the bed's condim 4 with a rescheduled coefficient. Difference: Hertz pressure shape and a geometric a; no area or centre of pressure. The bed's condim 4 uses the Winkler arm (8/15)·a, fitted to Drake.
- NVIDIA PhysX/Isaac: torsionalPatchRadius and patch friction; Narang et al., 'Factory: Fast contact for robotic assembly', RSS 2022 (2022) https://arxiv.org/abs/2205.03532: PhysX gives torsional friction through a fixed patch radius per shape, and patch friction applies friction impulses at up to two anchors per contact patch. Factory adds GPU SDF collisions with contact reduction (1,000 nut–bolt pairs in real time). Overlap: How GPU simulators used for in-hand RL handle torsion and patches. Difference: A fixed radius or two anchors, not calibrated to a pressure law.
- Ciocarlie, Lackner, Allen, 'Soft finger model with adaptive contact geometry for grasping and manipulation tasks', WHC 2007 (2007) https://roam.me.columbia.edu/sites/default/files/content/papers/haptics07_contact.pdf: Builds a soft-finger contact from local surface curvatures and elastic-contact theory, with coupled tangential and torsional friction constraints solved as an LCP in GraspIt!. Overlap: Friction torque derived from the patch's pressure distribution, coupled to tangential friction; the pads produce this coupling through per-sphere cones. Difference: An analytic limit surface on a single contact for quasi-static grasp analysis. The pads are dynamic multi-point contacts in a GPU simulator.
- Xydas, Kao, 'Modeling of contact mechanics and friction limit surfaces for soft fingers in robotics, with experimental results', IJRR 18(9), 1999; Kao, Cutkosky, IJRR 11(1), 1992; Howe, Cutkosky, IJRR 15(6), 1996; Goyal, Ruina, Papadopoulos, Wear 143, 1991 (1991–1999) https://researchconnect.stonybrook.edu/en/publications/modeling-of-contact-mechanics-and-friction-limit-surfaces-for-sof/: Power law a ∝ N^γ with γ = n/(2n+1) between 0 and 1/3 (Hertz 1/3), measured on rubber and silicone fingertips. Friction limit surfaces approximated as ellipses in force and moment. Overlap: The reference for the arm–load exponent test; the Winkler value 1/4 lies inside the measured range. Difference: The pads fix γ = 1/4 for a sphere tip. No exponent has been measured on the printed TPU tip.
- Inoue, Hirai, 'Elastic model of deformable fingertip for soft-fingered manipulation', IEEE T-RO 22(6), 2006; 'Mechanics and Control of Soft-fingered Manipulation', Springer 2009 (2006) https://www.springer.com/gp/book/9781848009806: A parallel distributed model of independent virtual springs for a hemispherical soft fingertip. Elastic potential, a local minimum of elastic energy for rolling stability, and experiments with rubber tips. Overlap: A Winkler-type soft fingertip used for manipulation and control analysis. Difference: An analytic planar model for controller design; no simulator implementation and no sampled friction torque.
- Weller, Zachmann, 'Inner sphere trees for proximity and penetration queries', RSS 2009; McNeely, Puterbaugh, Troy, 'Six degree-of-freedom haptic rendering using voxel sampling', SIGGRAPH 1999; Barbič, James, IEEE Trans. Haptics 1(1), 2008 (1999–2009) https://roboticsproceedings.org/rss05/p10.html: Sphere packings inside objects give penetration-volume penalty forces and torques at haptic rates. Voxmap-pointshell and point-shell-on-distance-field methods give distributed penalty forces and torques from sampled points. Overlap: Sampled geometry (spheres or points) whose summed penalty forces produce the contact torque. Difference: Haptic rendering with penalty forces not tied to a material law. Inner sphere trees fill the volume; the pads sit on the surface with per-site stiffness inside a constraint solver.
- Höhner, Wirtz, Kruggel-Emden, Scherer, 'Comparison of the multi-sphere and polyhedral approach to simulate non-spherical particles within the discrete element method', Powder Technology 208(3), 2011 (2011) https://arxiv.org/pdf/1903.10281: Multi-sphere DEM: when a clumped particle makes several sphere contacts, the force evolution changes and the contact becomes over-stiff. A known correction divides each step's force increment by the number of active contacts. Overlap: Stiffness that grows with the number of touching spheres. Difference: DEM corrects this away; the pads set each sphere's stiffness by its area so that the sum samples a foundation.
- Macklin, Müller, Chentanez, Kim, 'Unified particle physics for real-time applications' (NVIDIA Flex), ACM TOG 33(4), 2014 (2014) https://hgpu.org/?p=12166: Rigid and deformable bodies as particle clusters with per-particle contacts, solved by position-based dynamics on the GPU. Overlap: Friction torque from the spread of per-particle contacts on the GPU. Difference: PBD stiffness is not a material modulus; no pressure law and no calibration.
- Akinola, Xu, Carius, Fox, Narang, 'TacSL: A library for visuotactile sensor simulation and learning', IEEE T-RO 2025 (2025) https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.06506: Penalty tactile force field at the sensor's tactile points from SDF queries: f_n = (−k_n + k_d·ḋ)·d·n, with tangential force capped at μ·f_n. Computed as a post-processing step; the dynamics use a Kelvin–Voigt contact. 1.54 M FPS at 10×10 resolution with 32,768 envs. Overlap: Many penalty points sampling a contact surface, batched on the GPU. Difference: A sensor output only. The pads' forces drive the dynamics.
- Kasolowsky, Bäuml, 'Fine manipulation using a tactile skin: learning in simulation and sim-to-real transfer', 2024 (2024) https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.12735: A linear local penetration law σ = E·ε on 14,884 points of a soft fingertip skin, solved for equilibrium with PyBullet's normal force. E is fitted to indentation data (236–848 MPa/m). Marble and bolt rolling transfer to the DLR Hand II. Overlap: A Winkler law on a fingertip, calibrated against measurement. Difference: A sensor model; PyBullet's point contact drives the dynamics. The pads realise the law in the dynamics but have not been calibrated against a measurement.
- Leins, Patzelt, Haschke, 'HydroelasticTouch: Simulation of tactile sensors with hydroelastic contact surfaces', 2025 (MuJoCo plugin ubi-agni/mujoco_contact_surfaces) (2025) https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.08077: Drake-style hydroelastic contact surfaces inside MuJoCo through a plugin, with per-face forces applied as passive forces and taxels sampled from the surface. Zero-shot sim-to-real object-angle estimation. Overlap: Hydroelastic friction torque in MuJoCo. Difference: CPU only, applied outside the constraint solver as explicit forces, and creeps at the stiction tolerance. The pads stay in the solver and run on MuJoCo-Warp.
- Dang, Lee, Mukadam, Wu, Bucher, Nambi, Fazeli, 'HydroShear: Hydroelastic shear simulation for tactile sim-to-real reinforcement learning', RSS 2026 (2026) https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.00446: Hydroelastic contact plus SDF-tracked surface points with stick–slip and path-dependent shear for GelSight sensors. 93 % average zero-shot success against 34 % for tactile images and 58–61 % for other shear simulators. Overlap: The tangential state the pads lack. The colleague's advice of 2026-10-13 cites it for matching tactile and contact models. Difference: A tactile sensor model independent of the physics engine. The pads keep no tangential state.
- DiffTactile (Si et al., ICLR 2024); Tacchi (Chen et al., RA-L 2023); Taccel (Li, Du et al., NeurIPS 2025); TACTO (Wang et al., RA-L 2022) (2022–2025) https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12908: Soft-fingertip and tactile simulators: FEM with penalty contact (DiffTactile), MLS-MPM (Tacchi), IPC with affine body dynamics at 915 FPS for 4,096 envs (Taccel), and rendering on PyBullet point contacts (TACTO). Overlap: Distributed contact on soft fingertips for manipulation learning. Difference: Deformable-body cost, or rendering only. The pads are a rigid-body sampling at about 1 µs per world-step.
- Genesis, SAP coupler with hydroelastic contact (2025) https://genesis-world.readthedocs.io/en/latest/user_guide/theory/couplers/sap_coupler.html: A Drake-derived SAP coupler for rigid–FEM contact with hydroelastic contact (default stiffness 1e8); 64-bit only, implicit FEM required. Overlap: An alternative GPU hydroelastic path. Difference: Built for FEM coupling and requires 64-bit; not run here.
- MuJoCo Menagerie hand and gripper models (Franka Panda, Wonik Allegro, Shadow Hand) and MuJoCo Playground LEAP hand (Zakka et al., 2025) (2022–2025) https://github.com/google-deepmind/mujoco_menagerie: Panda: five box pad geoms per finger at default solref and solimp. Allegro: one capsule per tip, elliptic cone, impratio 10. Shadow: one mesh per distal link. Playground LEAP (leap_rh_mjx.xml): one box per tip at condim 3 against the cube; the cube reorientation transferred to hardware (arXiv 2502.08844). Overlap: Several geoms per fingertip pad (Panda) for grasp stability. Difference: None is calibrated to a pressure law. LEAP's sim-to-real cube reorientation works with condim-3 single-box tips, a counterexample a reviewer may raise.
- Qi et al., 'In-hand object rotation via rapid motor adaptation' (HORA), CoRL 2022; Mandi et al., 'DexMachina', 2025 (2022–2025) https://arxiv.org/abs/2210.04887: HORA: Isaac Gym Allegro hand with modified fingertip geometry, randomized friction, 16,384 envs, zero-shot transfer. DexMachina: Genesis with convex-decomposition collision meshes; 'more careful tuning with real reference hardware will be needed'. Overlap: High-throughput in-hand RL, the owner's use case. Difference: Rigid point contacts; no friction-torque calibration and no pressure law.
- Le Lidec, Jallet, Montaut, Laptev, Schmid, Carpentier, 'Contact models in robotics: a comparative analysis', IEEE T-RO 40, 2024 (2024) https://arxiv.org/abs/2304.06372: Benchmarks the contact models and relaxations of robotics simulators (MuJoCo, Drake and others) for physical and computational accuracy. Overlap: The methodology for comparing models against analytic limits. Difference: Contains no distributed fingertip models.
- Nechyporenko, Zhang, Campbell, Roncone, 'MorphIt: Flexible spherical approximation of robot morphology', 2025 (2025) https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.14061: Gradient-based sphere approximations of robot geometry that trade fidelity against cost. This repository's CAD finger uses MorphIt-B spheres. Overlap: Sphere sets on robot links; the geometry front end that CSLC builds on. Difference: Collision geometry only, with no per-sphere stiffness.
- Kurtz, Lin, 'Contact-implicit trajectory optimization with hydroelastic contact and iLQR', IROS 2022 (2022) https://arxiv.org/abs/2202.13986: Hydroelastic contact inside iLQR trajectory optimization, replayed open loop on a Kinova Gen3 arm. Overlap: Patch contact used for planning and control. Difference: Drake on CPU. The pads target GPU RL and MPC throughput in MuJoCo.

## Deck 23 intuitions missing from the overview (deck reader)

### spring-bed, spring-bed-force, spring-bed-arm, hydroelastic-is-a-bed, hertz (importance 5) -> Step 2: replaces the sentence before eq. (2), eq. (2) and the line 'Eliminating delta0 gives the arm as a power of the load' before eq. (3); the last sentence bridges to step 3

The quarter power follows from two proportionalities: on a bed the patch area and the mean pressure each grow with depth, so F grows as delta0^2 while the patch radius grows as delta0^(1/2); under Hertz the mean pressure grows with the strain a/R, so F grows as delta^(3/2) and a as F^(1/3). Drake's field is such a bed because its pressure depends on the local depth of the undeformed sphere alone.

Math: R-\sqrt{R^2-\rho^2}\approx\frac{\rho^2}{2R};\ \delta(\rho)=\delta_0-\frac{\rho^2}{2R},\ a=\sqrt{2R\delta_0};\ F=2\pi k_w\int_0^a\Bigl(\delta_0-\frac{\rho^2}{2R}\Bigr)\rho\,d\rho=2\pi k_w\Bigl(\frac{\delta_0a^2}{2}-\frac{a^4}{8R}\Bigr)=2\pi k_w\Bigl(R\delta_0^2-\frac{R\delta_0^2}{2}\Bigr)=\pi k_wR\delta_0^2;\ a^4=4R^2\delta_0^2=\frac{4RF}{\pi k_w};\ \bar r=\frac{8}{15}a=\frac{8}{15}\Bigl(\frac{4R}{\pi k_w}\Bigr)^{1/4}F^{1/4}=1.035\,F^{1/4}\,\mathrm{mm};\ \text{Hertz: }a^2=R\delta,\ \bar p=\frac{4E^*}{3\pi}\frac aR\Rightarrow F=\frac43E^*\sqrt R\,\delta^{3/2},\ a^3=\frac{3FR}{4E^*}

Proposed text: A sphere's surface at distance \(\rho\) from its lowest point lies \(R-\sqrt{R^2-\rho^2}\approx\rho^2/2R\) higher, so the spring there is compressed by \(\delta_0-\rho^2/2R\) and the patch ends at \(a=\sqrt{2R\delta_0}\). Summed over rings, with \(a^2=2R\delta_0\) substituted,
\[F=2\pi k_w\Bigl(\frac{\delta_0a^2}{2}-\frac{a^4}{8R}\Bigr)=2\pi k_w\Bigl(R\delta_0^2-\frac{R\delta_0^2}{2}\Bigr)=\pi k_wR\,\delta_0^2 .\]
The force grows as \(\delta_0^2\) because the patch area \(2\pi R\delta_0\) and the mean pressure \(k_w\delta_0/2\) each grow in proportion to \(\delta_0\). The pressure \(k_w\delta_0(1-\rho^2/a^2)\) is the paraboloid of step 1, so \(\bar r=8a/15\), and eliminating \(\delta_0\),
\[a^4=4R^2\delta_0^2=\frac{4RF}{\pi k_w},\qquad \bar r=\frac{8}{15}\Bigl(\frac{4R}{\pi k_w}\Bigr)^{1/4}F^{1/4},\qquad T=\mu\,c\,F^{5/4}.\]
For the fingertip, \(k_w=E/R\) = 0.948 N/mm³: 1 N sinks the sphere 178 µm into a flat face, the patch has a radius of 1.94 mm and a peak pressure of 0.169 MPa, and the contact stiffness \(dF/d\delta_0=2\pi k_wR\delta_0\) is 11.2 N/mm. Drake's field is such a bed because Drake computes the pressure at each point of the contact surface from that point's depth inside the undeformed sphere alone. In an elastic solid the patch at the same approach is smaller, \(a^2=R\delta\), and the mean pressure grows with the strain \(a/R\), so \(F\propto\delta^{3/2}\) and \(a\propto F^{1/3}\) (step 3).

### recap-soft-constraint, rest-spring, rest-spring-live, neighbours-cancel (importance 5) -> Step 6: between eq. (9) and eq. (10), replacing 'The constrained acceleration is ... independently, and'; after eq. (10), replacing 'The coupling through A ... cancels at rest' and 'relaxes in 2t_c = 20 ms, twice Drake's 10 ms on the bed'

MuJoCo's constraint equation reads R f = a_ref - a1 row by row for any Delassus matrix A, so the dense coupling between spheres acts only through the constrained acceleration a1, which is zero at rest; away from rest one row moves as a critically damped system with time constant t_c whatever its stiffness.

Math: (A+R)f=a_\text{ref}-a_0,\ a_1=a_0+Af\Rightarrow Rf=a_\text{ref}-a_1\ (\text{any }A);\ \text{rest: }a_1=0,\ v=0\Rightarrow R_if_i=-k\,r_i;\ d_\text{max}=d_0,\ \zeta=1:\ k=\frac{1}{d_0t_c^2},\ R_i=\frac{1-d_0}{d_0}\hat\Lambda_i\Rightarrow k_s=\frac{k}{R_i}=\frac{1}{(1-d_0)\hat\Lambda_it_c^2};\ \text{one row, }A=\hat\Lambda:\ f=\frac{d_0(a_\text{ref}-a_0)}{\hat\Lambda},\ a_1=d_0a_\text{ref}+(1-d_0)a_0\Rightarrow\ddot r+\frac{2}{t_c}\dot r+\frac{r}{t_c^2}=(1-d_0)a_0;\ r-r_\infty\propto(1+t/t_c)e^{-t/t_c},\ 5\%\text{ at }4.74\,t_c;\ r_\infty=(1-d_0)t_c^2a_0=F/k_s

Proposed text: Equation (9) holds row by row for every force inside its cone, whatever \(A\) is: with the constrained acceleration \(a_1=a_0+Af\) it reads \(Rf=a_\text{ref}-a_1\), and \(R\) is diagonal because MuJoCo builds each \(R_i\) from its own \(\hat\Lambda_i\). The coupling between rows enters only through \(a_1\). At rest every row has \(a_1=0\) and \(v=0\), so \(R_if_i=-k\,r_i\), and with \(k=1/(d_0t_c^2)\) and \(R_i=(1-d_0)\hat\Lambda_i/d_0\) this gives (10). The 13 spheres of a 1 mm pad pressed on a plate at 1 N belong to one body on a slide joint, so every entry of \(A\) is 90.74 kg\(^{-1}\) and \(A\) has rank one, with eigenvalue 1,180 kg\(^{-1}\). While the pad moves, pressing one sphere harder accelerates the body and unloads the others; at rest each sphere's force over its overlap is 820.96 N/m whatever the others carry. Away from rest, one row with \(A=\hat\Lambda\) has \(a_1=d_0a_\text{ref}+(1-d_0)a_0\):
\[\ddot r+\frac{2}{t_c}\,\dot r+\frac{r}{t_c^2}=(1-d_0)\,a_0 ,\]
a critically damped motion with time constant \(t_c\) for any \(d_0\) and \(\hat\Lambda\). A sphere pushed 20 µm from rest returns as \((1+t/t_c)\,e^{-t/t_c}\) and is within 5 % of rest after \(4.74\,t_c\), 47 ms at \(t_c\) = 10 ms. The ratio \(c_i/k_s=2t_c\) in (10) is the normal row's damping over its rest stiffness under steady loading, 20 ms against Drake's dissipation time of 10 ms.

### creep-mechanism, creep-live, pads-no-presliding (importance 5) -> Step 8: replaces the derivation before eq. (12) and CREEP_TEXT (scripts/contact_overview_page.py:565-593)

A friction row has damping and no spring, so each touching sphere is a damper of coefficient c = 2 kappa k_s t_c, kappa times the normal row's; a held load slides the pad at the speed where the dampers carry it, faster than the equal-share value because the outer spheres reach their cones first, and a finer pad does not slow it.

Math: a_\text{ref}=-b\,v\ (\text{no }k);\ a_1=0\Rightarrow R_tf_t=-b\,v;\ R_t=\frac{R_n}{\kappa}=\frac{(1-d_0)\hat\Lambda}{d_0\kappa},\ b=\frac{2}{d_0t_c}\Rightarrow f_t=-\frac{2\kappa}{(1-d_0)\hat\Lambda t_c}v=-2\kappa k_st_c\,v;\ c=2\times100\times820.96\times0.01=1641.9\,\mathrm{N\,s/m};\ v=\frac{F_t}{nc}\ (\text{equal shares});\ F_t=\sum_i\min(c\,v,\ \mu f_{n,i})\ (\text{cones});\ nk_s\approx k_w\pi a^2;\ u(t)=\frac{1}{nc}\int_0^tF_t\,dt

Proposed text: At steady sliding \(a_1=0\), so each friction row obeys \(R_tf_t=-b\,v\). With \(R_t=R_n/\kappa\) and (10) this is a damper with \(\kappa\) times the normal row's coefficient:
\[f_t=-\frac{b}{R_t}\,v=-\frac{2\kappa}{(1-d_0)\hat\Lambda t_c}\,v=-c\,v,\qquad c=2\kappa\,k_s\,t_c ,\]
1,641.9 N s/m per sphere for the 1 mm pad at \(\kappa\) = 100. Once \(d_0\) is chosen for \(k_s\), \((1-d_0)\hat\Lambda=1/(k_st_c^2)\) and neither \(d_0\) nor \(\hat\Lambda\) remains: the bed's pad at 0.866 and 90.74 kg\(^{-1}\) and the thumb's at 0.741 and 47.2 kg\(^{-1}\) have the same \(c\) to within 0.5 %. All touching spheres slide together, so with equal shares the pad creeps at \(v=F_t/(nc)\), (12). Rigid Coulomb friction leaves the shares undetermined below slip; MuJoCo's dampers make them equal, so the outer spheres, with the smallest normal forces, reach \(\mu f_{n,i}\) first and the inner ones carry the rest:
\[F_t=\sum_i\min\bigl(c\,v,\ \mu f_{n,i}\bigr).\]
At half the slip force this raises the creep from the equal-share 19.0, 27.7 and 43.5 µm/s at 0.5, 1 and 3 N to 22.7, 30.8 and 53.3 µm/s, with 6, 6 and 12 spheres at their cones, the bed's measured values to four digits (creep.jsonl). A finer pad creeps as fast, because \(nk_s\) stays near \(k_w\) times the patch area, 18.1 N/mm for the 1 mm pad and 19.6 N/mm for the 0.5 mm pad at 1 N; \(\kappa\) and \(t_c\) set the speed. At the policy trainer's impratio of 10 the pinch creeps 0.30 mm/s at 1 N per pad. A damper moves the tool by the time integral of its load, \(u=\int F_t\,dt/(nc)\), so the first 0.5 s ramp of task 8 (0.25 N s) moves the tool 6.9 µm and the tool does not return when the load does.

### explicit-damping, pad-step-bound, step-bound-data (importance 5) -> Step 9: replaces the text from 'MuJoCo evaluates a_ref with the velocity at the start of the step' through eq. (14) and the sentence 'For the 1 mm pads (d0 = 0.74-0.89, t_c = 10-15 ms) the bound is 7-13 ms...'; Figure 6's diagonal and stab_bound() in scripts/contact_overview_page.py:637 follow it

MuJoCo evaluates both the spring and the damper of a_ref at the start of the step, so the stability condition of the semi-implicit step includes the spring term h^2 omega^2, which lowers the pad bound from q t_c to 2 t_c (sqrt(1+q) - 1), 16 % lower for the 13-sphere pad.

Math: \ddot x=-c\dot x-\omega^2x;\ v_{k+1}=(1-hc)v_k-h\omega^2x_k,\ x_{k+1}=x_k+hv_{k+1};\ M=\begin{bmatrix}1-h^2\omega^2 & h(1-hc)\\ -h\omega^2 & 1-hc\end{bmatrix},\ \det M=1-hc,\ \operatorname{tr}M=2-hc-h^2\omega^2;\ |\lambda_{1,2}|<1\iff hc<2,\ h^2\omega^2+2hc<4;\ \text{one row: }c=\frac2{t_c},\ \omega^2=\frac1{t_c^2}\Rightarrow h<2(\sqrt2-1)t_c=0.828\,t_c;\ n\text{ rows: }d'=\frac{nd_0}{nd_0+1-d_0},\ q=\frac{d_0}{d'}=d_0+\frac{1-d_0}{n},\ c=\frac{2}{qt_c},\ \omega^2=\frac{1}{qt_c^2}\Rightarrow\Bigl(\frac h{t_c}\Bigr)^2+4\frac h{t_c}<4q\Rightarrow h<2t_c(\sqrt{1+q}-1);\ \text{friction rows (bed rows, untested): }c=\frac{\lambda_t}{\lambda_t+R_t}\,b\Rightarrow h<t_c\Bigl(d_0+\frac{(1-d_0)\hat\Lambda}{\kappa\lambda_t}\Bigr)\approx d_0t_c

Proposed text: MuJoCo evaluates \(a_\text{ref}=-b\,v-k\,r\) at the state at the start of the step, so the spring term is as explicit as the damper. For \(\ddot x=-c\,\dot x-\omega^2x\), updating the velocity and then the position with the new velocity gives
\[\begin{bmatrix}x\\ v\end{bmatrix}_{k+1}=\begin{bmatrix}1-h^2\omega^2 & h(1-hc)\\ -h\omega^2 & 1-hc\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}x\\ v\end{bmatrix}_k ,\]
whose eigenvalues lie inside the unit circle when \(hc<2\) and \(h^2\omega^2+2hc<4\). One row with \(A=\hat\Lambda\), \(c=2/t_c\) and \(\omega^2=1/t_c^2\) is stable for \(h<2(\sqrt2-1)\,t_c=0.828\,t_c\). For \(n\) rows moving together, (13) gives \(c=d'b=2/(q\,t_c)\) and \(\omega^2=d'k=1/(q\,t_c^2)\) with \(q=d_0/d'=d_0+(1-d_0)/n\), and the second condition becomes \((h/t_c)^2+4h/t_c<4q\):
\[\Delta t<2t_c\bigl(\sqrt{1+q}-1\bigr)\ \ge\ 2t_c\bigl(\sqrt{1+d_0}-1\bigr).\]
The damper condition alone, \(\Delta t<q\,t_c\), is 5–20 % longer. For the 13 touching spheres of a 1 mm pad on a plate (\(d_0\) = 0.8658, \(t_c\) = 10 ms, \(q\) = 0.8761) the bound is 7.394 ms against the damper's 8.761 ms, and the spectral radius of MuJoCo's one-step map crosses 1 at 7.394 ms; at 24 pairs of \(t_c\) and \(d_0\) the measured threshold equals the bound to \(10^{-4}t_c\). For the 1 mm pads of the hand (\(d_0\) = 0.74–0.89, \(t_c\) = 10–15 ms) the bound is at least 6.4–11.2 ms. Damping on the pad's own joint, which implicitfast integrates implicitly, raises the threshold: 5 N s/m keeps the plate pad stable to \(2t_c\) at \(d_0\ge0.5\). In bed task 7 the tool hangs on friction rows, which carry no spring; their damper alone gives \(\Delta t<t_c\bigl(d_0+(1-d_0)\hat\Lambda/(\kappa\lambda_t)\bigr)\approx d_0t_c\), with \(\lambda_t\) the largest eigenvalue of the friction rows' \(A\), and this bound brackets 18 of the 21 pad pairs and the point contact's failures at 3, 7 and 10 ms for \(d_0\) = 0.2, 0.5 and 0.9, which no normal-row bound predicts. Which mode fails first in the pinch is open (Future work).

### hinge-demo, point-is-hinge, swing-task (importance 4) -> Contact mechanics of a soft fingertip, step 1: after the paragraph that follows eq. (1); the Brake paragraph of Comparison with the reference models then cites it

The friction arm decides a task outcome through one torque balance: two pads hold a pinched tool where 2 mu F rbar(F) >= mgd cos(phi), and point contacts (rbar = 0) hold it only hanging.

Math: mgd=0.0245\times9.81\times0.015=3.61\,\mathrm{mN\,m};\quad T_\text{max}=2\mu F\,\bar r(F),\ \bar r=0.974\,F^{1/4}\,\mathrm{mm};\quad F=1\,\mathrm N:\ T_\text{max}=1.95\,\mathrm{mN\,m}<mgd;\quad 2\mu F_h\,\bar r(F_h)=mgd\Rightarrow F_h^{5/4}=\frac{3.61}{2\times0.974}\Rightarrow F_h=1.64\,\mathrm N;\quad \varphi=\arccos\frac{2\mu F\bar r(F)}{mgd}=57^\circ\,(1\,\mathrm N),\ 26^\circ\,(1.5\,\mathrm N),\ 85.9^\circ\,(0.2\,\mathrm N)

Proposed text: Two point contacts pinching a tool form a free hinge about the line through them. The real_v1 screwdriver weighs 0.240 N with its centre of mass \(d\) = 15 mm from that line, so gravity exerts \(mgd\cos\varphi\) about it, with \(mgd\) = 3.61 mN m and \(\varphi\) the tool's angle below horizontal. Two pads hold the tool at rest where
\[2\mu F\,\bar r(F)\ \ge\ mgd\cos\varphi .\]
At 1 N per pad the law of step 2 gives \(\bar r\) = 0.974 mm and a capacity of 1.95 mN m, so the tool turns to \(\varphi=\arccos(1.95/3.61)\) = 57°; it stays horizontal from 1.64 N per pad. Point contacts have \(\bar r=0\) and hold the tool only hanging, at 90°. Bed task 5 lowers the pinch to 0.2 N, where the pads still transmit 0.26 mN m and the balance gives 85.9°; the patch models end at 87.3–88.3° after overshooting, and point contact swings to 175°. MuJoCo's pads never hold the tool still: at 2 N per pad it turns 7° in 2 s and still turns 2.7°/s after 8 s, the creep of step 8.

### friction-arm, arm-uniform, arm-shapes (importance 4) -> Step 1, between eq. (1) and the sentence 'T is the largest torque...'; it supplies the 8/15 used in eq. (2) and the 3 pi/16 used in eq. (4)

The arm is a force-weighted mean distance from the patch centre, so its ratio to the patch radius depends only on the pressure's shape: more load near the rim gives a longer arm, from a/2 for a cone through 8a/15 for a spring bed and 3 pi a/16 for Hertz to a for a ring at the rim.

Math: dA=2\pi\rho\,d\rho;\ F=\int_0^a p\,2\pi\rho\,d\rho=p\,\pi a^2,\ T=\mu\int_0^a p\,\rho\,2\pi\rho\,d\rho=\mu p\,\frac{2\pi a^3}{3}\Rightarrow\bar r=\frac{2a}{3};\ p_0(1-\rho^2/a^2):\ \bar r=\frac{2\pi a^3(\frac13-\frac15)}{2\pi a^2(\frac12-\frac14)}=\frac{8a}{15};\ p_0\sqrt{1-\rho^2/a^2}:\ \bar r=a\,\frac{\int_0^1u^2\sqrt{1-u^2}\,du}{\int_0^1u\sqrt{1-u^2}\,du}=a\,\frac{\pi/16}{1/3}=\frac{3\pi a}{16};\ \text{cone }1-u:\ \frac a2;\ \text{ring at the rim}:\ a

Proposed text: The friction arm is the force-weighted mean distance from the patch centre. Friction on opposite sides of the centre turns the tool the same way, so a patch whose centre of pressure lies at its centre still has an arm. Summing over rings of area \(2\pi\rho\,d\rho\), a disc of radius \(a\) under a uniform pressure \(p\) gives
\[F=p\,\pi a^2,\qquad T=\mu p\,\frac{2\pi a^3}{3},\qquad \bar r=\frac{2a}{3},\]
from \(\int_0^a\rho\,d\rho=a^2/2\) and \(\int_0^a\rho^2\,d\rho=a^3/3\). The size of the patch scales every lever and the shape of the pressure sets the weights. With \(u=\rho/a\), a cone \(1-u\) gives \(\bar r/a\) = 1/2, the paraboloid \(1-u^2\) of step 2 gives 8/15 = 0.533, the Hertz profile \(\sqrt{1-u^2}\) of step 3 gives \(3\pi/16\) = 0.589, a uniform pressure 2/3 and a ring at the rim 1. A 2 mm disc pressed with 1 N at uniform pressure transmits up to 1.333 mN m with \(\mu=1\).

### which-law, check-compliance (importance 4) -> Step 3, after eq. (5), replacing 'The ratio is independent of the modulus...'; the Future work item 'Hertz or foundation on the printed tip' points to it

The ratio of the arms at two loads depends only on the exponent, so the modulus cancels; a bench measures torque, whose ratio is the force ratio times the arm ratio, and an exponent below 1/4 fits neither law.

Math: \bar r=cF^k\Rightarrow\frac{\bar r(F_2)}{\bar r(F_1)}=\Bigl(\frac{F_2}{F_1}\Bigr)^k;\ 6^{1/4}=1.565,\ 6^{1/3}=1.817;\ \frac{T(3\,\mathrm N)}{T(0.5\,\mathrm N)}=6^{1+k}=9.39\ \text{(bed)},\ 10.90\ \text{(Hertz)};\ \text{two pads: }T=2\mu F\bar r,\ F_\text{slip}=2\mu F\Rightarrow\bar r=T/F_\text{slip};\ k=0.15:\ 6^{0.15}=1.31

Proposed text: The modulus cancels in (5) because a power law has a fixed prefactor: \(\bar r(F_2)/\bar r(F_1)=(F_2/F_1)^k\) for any \(c\). A bench measures torque, \(T=\mu F\bar r\propto F^{1+k}\), so from 0.5 to 3 N the torque grows 9.39 times on a bed and 10.90 times under Hertz, 16 % apart. The arm is the spin torque divided by the pull force at slip under the same pinch, \(\bar r=T/F_\text{slip}\) for two pads, which removes a friction coefficient that varies with load. Drake's onset arm grows 1.52 times over that range and the 1 mm pad's 1.52 at onset and 1.54 while spinning, close to the bed's 1.565, since both integrate a bed. A tip whose contact radius grows as \(F^{0.15}\), inside the range of 0 to 1/3 that Xydas and Kao measured on soft fingertips, gives 1.31, which neither law fits. The test assumes a spherical tip: on the printed TPU block the screwdriver presses a line across the flat face, and the finite-element arm grows only 1.02–1.11 times from 0.5 to 3 N (docs/experiments/20261009-tpu_tip_fem/tip_runs_surf.jsonl).

### rest-spring, inverse-inertia, choose-d (importance 4) -> End of step 6 ('Solving (10) for the impedance...') and the first sentences of step 7 (MASS_TEXT, scripts/contact_overview_page.py:540-560)

Solref's stiffness is an acceleration per metre, so the force stiffness carries the effective mass 1/Lambda; at a given t_c the softest spring MuJoCo can give is 1/(Lambda t_c^2), which sets the lower bound on t_c, and a tool-mass error matters most when the pad is heavy relative to the tool.

Math: [k]=\mathrm{s^{-2}},\ [R_i]=\mathrm{kg^{-1}}\Rightarrow[k/R_i]=\mathrm{N/m};\ d_0=1-\frac{1}{k_s\hat\Lambda t_c^2}\in(0,1)\iff t_c>(k_s\hat\Lambda)^{-1/2};\ \lim_{d_0\to0}\frac{1}{(1-d_0)\hat\Lambda t_c^2}=\frac{1}{\hat\Lambda t_c^2};\ \hat\Lambda=w_p+\frac1m;\ \frac{k_s(m)}{k_s(m_0)}=\frac{w_p+1/m_0}{w_p+1/m}:\ \frac{90.74}{60.19}=1.51\ (\text{bed}),\ \frac{47.2}{16.66}=2.83\ (\text{thumb});\ \bar r\propto k_s^{-1/4}:\ 1.51^{-1/4}=0.902,\ 2.83^{-1/4}=0.771

Proposed text: Solref's \(k\) is an acceleration per metre, in s\(^{-2}\); dividing it by \(R_i\), in kg\(^{-1}\), gives a force per metre, which is how the bodies' masses enter (10). Solved for the impedance,
\[d_0=1-\frac{1}{k_s\hat\Lambda t_c^2},\qquad 0<d_0<1\iff t_c>(k_s\hat\Lambda)^{-1/2}.\]
At a given \(t_c\) the softest spring MuJoCo can produce is \(1/(\hat\Lambda t_c^2)\), reached as \(d_0\to0\), so a softer sphere needs a longer time constant. On the bed's 20 g railed pads, \(\hat\Lambda\) = 50 + 40.74 = 90.74 kg\(^{-1}\), the 1 mm pad's 820.96 N/m needs \(t_c>\) 3.66 ms and takes \(d_0\) = 0.866 at 10 ms; on the hand's thumb, \(\hat\Lambda\) = 6.47 + 40.74 = 47.2 kg\(^{-1}\), it needs 5.1 ms and takes 0.741. \(\hat\Lambda\) is the pad's inverse weight \(w_p\) plus the tool's, so with \(d_0\) held, a tool \(s\) times heavier scales every sphere's stiffness by \((w_p+1/m_0)/(w_p+1/(s\,m_0))\): 1.51 on the bed and 2.83 on the thumb for a fourfold tool, whose arm then shortens by 10 and 23 % through \(\bar r\propto k_s^{-1/4}\). MuJoCo computes body_invweight0 once, at qpos0, and does not update it as the bodies move, so \(d_0\) read once after compiling gives every sphere its target stiffness.

### pad-spheres, sphere-stiffness, pad-sampler (importance 3) -> Step 5, after eq. (8), replacing the sentences from 'The 1 mm pad on a 45 deg cap has n = 205...' to '...within 1 %'

The pad's contact points lie on the inner radius R - r_s, which raises their density on the tool by (R/(R - r_s))^2, the factor in eq. (8), and makes the pad a bed of curvature R - r_s whose static arm is ((R - r_s)/R)^(1/4) = 0.982 of the bed's; resolution depends on the spacing against the patch radius, and a coarse pad's arm depends on where its spheres land.

Math: A_\text{cap}=2\pi R^2(1-\cos35^\circ)=126.5\,\mathrm{mm^2},\ n=126,\ A_s=1.0038\,\mathrm{mm^2};\ \text{contact at }(R-r_s)\sin\theta_i\Rightarrow\text{density}\times\Bigl(\frac{R}{R-r_s}\Bigr)^2=1.159;\ k_s=k_wA_s\Bigl(\frac{R-r_s}{R}\Bigr)^2=0.948\times0.8661=0.821\,\mathrm{N/mm};\ \text{overlap }\delta_0-(R-r_s)(1-\cos\theta_i)\approx\delta_0-\frac{\rho_i^2}{2(R-r_s)}\Rightarrow\frac{\bar r_\text{pad}}{\bar r_\text{bed}}=\Bigl(\frac{R-r_s}{R}\Bigr)^{1/4}=0.982

Proposed text: The factor \(((R-r_s)/R)^2\) in (8) comes from where the spheres touch. Each sphere meets the tool under its centre, at a lateral distance \((R-r_s)\sin\theta_i\) from the axis of the cap, so the lattice projected onto the tool shrinks by \((R-r_s)/R\) in each direction and its contact points are 1.159 times as dense as on the fingertip surface. The same geometry makes the pad a bed of curvature radius \(R-r_s\): sphere \(i\) overlaps the tool by \(\delta_0-\rho_i^2/2(R-r_s)\), so at equal force its arm is \(((R-r_s)/R)^{1/4}\) = 0.982 of the bed's. On a flat face at 1 N, 13 spheres of the bed's pad (126 spheres on a 35° cap, 820.96 N/m each; the hand's 45° cap holds 205 of 817 N/m) touch, and their arm is 1.019 mm against the bed's 1.035 mm and the predicted 1.016 mm. On the screwdriver the resting pad's arm is 0.802, 0.950 and 1.245 mm at 0.5, 1 and 3 N, 2.1–2.8 % under the law. Halving the spacing gives four times the spheres, each a quarter as stiff, so the stiffness per unit area stays \(k_w\). The spacing has to stay small against the patch radius, 1.6–2.6 mm at 0.5–3 N: at 0.5 N on a flat, rotating the lattice by 0–8° moves the 2 mm pad's arm from 46 % below to 4 % above the bed's, the 1 mm pad's from 7.3 % below to 0.7 % above and the 0.5 mm pad's from 2.7 to 1.3 % below.

### pad-limits, hydroelastic-is-a-bed, mindlin-presliding, against-drake (importance 3) -> Future work: the closing paragraph 'Drake and the spheres integrate the same pressure law...' and the item 'The modulus'

The pads inherit every omission of an uncoupled bed (lateral spread, shear before slip) and add MuJoCo's soft-friction creep; Drake's 10 MPa is a field parameter, so the printed tip's stiffness enters as k_w, which an indentation fixes better than a twist because the arm goes only as k_w^(-1/4).

Math: k_w=E/R\ (\text{field parameter});\ \bar r\propto k_w^{-1/4}\Rightarrow\frac{\Delta k_w}{k_w}\approx-4\,\frac{\Delta\bar r}{\bar r};\ \text{Hertz, }E=10\,\mathrm{MPa},\ \nu=0.45:\ E^*=\frac{E}{1-\nu^2}=12.5\,\mathrm{MPa},\ a=\Bigl(\frac{3FR}{4E^*}\Bigr)^{1/3}=0.86\,\mathrm{mm},\ \bar r=\frac{3\pi}{16}a=0.51\,\mathrm{mm}\ (1\,\mathrm N);\ \text{Mindlin: }\delta(Q)=\frac{3\mu F(2-\nu)}{16Ga}\Bigl[1-\Bigl(1-\frac{Q}{\mu F}\Bigr)^{2/3}\Bigr]=40.3\,\mu\mathrm m\ (F=1\,\mathrm N,\ Q=\mu F/2)

Proposed text: The pads sample an uncoupled bed through MuJoCo's soft friction, so they leave out what the bed leaves out and add creep. An elastic tip spreads load beyond the overlap, which the two-load test of step 3 detects on a spherical tip. An elastic tip also deflects before it slips, 40.3 µm at 1 N per pad and half the slip force by Mindlin's solution, and returns 38.0 µm of it; the pads and Drake store no tangential displacement. Drake's 10 MPa is a parameter of the pressure field, \(k_w=E/R\): a TPU of Young's modulus 10 MPa under Hertz (\(E^\ast\) = 12.5 MPa) gives an arm of 0.51 mm at 1 N against the bed's 1.035 mm. The finite-element fits of the planned prints give pad moduli of 6.9–16.5 MPa from the approach under the screwdriver (printed-tip page). Since the arm goes as \(k_w^{-1/4}\), a 10 % error in a measured arm is a 40 % error in \(k_w\), so the indentation fixes \(k_w\) and the twist checks the arm. The pad starts to spin 4–7 % below the law after turning 1.4–2.0°, and its spinning arm depends on the rate, 0.888 mm at 0.1 rad/s and 0.963 mm at 1 rad/s at 1 N, both through the friction dampers of step 8. A 1 mm pad misses surface features finer than 1 mm, and its arm at 0.5 N varies by 8 % with where its spheres land.


## Inconsistencies found by the readers

- [claims_records] Creep factor of the pads against Drake (Lede (contact_overview_text.lede: 155×); step 8 (contact_overview_page.creep_text: 177×); Drake section (refs_text, hardcoded 'about 100×'); Table 1 (cap_table, hardcoded 'Creeps 100× less'); bed page Future work ('150–260×'); deck 23 models-table ('150 times'); backends page ('155 and 145 times')): 100×, 155×, 177×, 150–260×, 150× -> Sphere tip, 1 ms, impratio 100 (pull_slip/twist_slip/shake.jsonl): pads/Drake 164/155/156× (translation, 0.5/1/3 N), 165/145/135× (twist), 150× (shake drift), so about 150×. 177× pools point contact (150–255×). At a 5 ms step 34× (Drake's creep grows with the step). On the TPU block 9× (translation) and 8× (twist) (tip_T1/T2.jsonl)
- [claims_records] Task-6 creep sentence uses pooled medians (Step 8 (contact_overview_page.creep_t6_text); copied into deck 23 creep-live notes): impratio 100→1000: 16.8→1.0 µm/s; ten noslip iterations: −0.54 µm/s -> mj_pads1, 1 N, 1 ms (creep.jsonl part T1): 30.76 → 3.08 (1000) → 1.03 (3000) → 0.31 µm/s (10,000), exactly 1/impratio. noslip 3 or 10 at impratio 100: −1.56 µm/s (−1.16/−2.70 at 0.5/3 N); noslip 1: −3.18; noslip 10 at impratio 1000: −0.16
- [claims_records] Figure 7 caption names the wrong fingertip for two of its columns (Template Figure 7 caption vs scripts/simulator_agreement_figure.data() (reads 20261005-contact_bed twist_slip and brake rows)): Caption: 'one fingertip shape (the printed TPU block, 2.7 mm fillets)' -> The twist and brake columns are the 10.55 mm sphere bed (Drake swing 87.3°); only the turn columns use the TPU block. Block values: onset arm 2.525 vs 2.496 mm (1 N), 2.736 vs 2.618 (3 N); brake force at 45° 0.338 vs 0.500 N
- [claims_records] Step 5 pad is not the bed's pad, and steps 6 and 8 quote the chain's parameters for the bed (Template steps 5, 6 and 8 vs scripts/hom_contact_rig.py (cap_deg default 35)): 205 spheres, 45° cap, 817 N/m, Λ̂ 47.2 1/kg, d0 0.741, minimum t_c 5.1/10.2 ms -> Bed: 126 spheres on a 35° cap, 820.96 N/m, Λ̂ 90.74 1/kg, d0 0.8658, minimum t_c 3.66/7.34 ms (deck 23 rest-spring, choose-d). (1−d0)Λ̂ = 12.2 in both, so the creep prediction is unaffected
- [claims_records] Resolution claim for the 2 mm pad (Step 5 ('13 % long ... 1 and 0.5 mm within 1 %')): +12.8 / −0.8 / −1.2 % at one placement -> Deck 23 math #2, 0–8° placement sweep: 2 mm −46.5 to +4.4 %, 1 mm −7.3 to +0.7 %, 0.5 mm −2.7 to −1.3 %. Bed twist of the 2 mm pad at 0.5 N: onset 0.380 mm (−54 %), sliding 0.559 mm (−32 %)
- [claims_records] Step-size bound (Step 9 eq (14), '7–13 ms', '19 of 21 bracket'): Δt < t_c(d0 + (1−d0)/n) -> Deck 23 pad-step-bound/step-bound-data: h < 2τ(√(1+q)−1) with q = d0 + (1−d0)/n, 5–20 % shorter, equal to the spectral threshold to 1e-4 τ. 1 mm pads (d0 0.74–0.89, t_c 10–15 ms): 6.4–11.2 ms. Bed rows bracket it in 10/21; the two (14) exceptions (t_c 20 ms, d0 0.9/0.95, fail at 15 ms) lie 1.4–6 % below its 15.2/15.9 ms. Point contact fails at d0·t_c, which neither form predicts
- [claims_records] Sphere 'relaxes in 2t_c' (Step 6; glossary 'relaxation time t_r ... a pad sphere's equivalent is 2t_c'): 20 ms, twice Drake's 10 ms -> 2t_c is the damper-to-spring ratio c/k. A pushed sphere returns critically damped as (1+t/τ)e^(−t/τ), within 5 % after 4.74 t_c = 47 ms (deck 23 review #1)
- [claims_records] body_invweight0 called independent of pose (Step 7 (mass_text)): 'independent of pose, so the load-time value is exact' -> MuJoCo computes it once at qpos0 and never updates it (deck 23 review #9). The load-time value equals the solver's but not the physical effective mass at another pose. mjlab mass randomisation (set_const) recomputes it and moves pad stiffness (contact sensitivity page)
- [claims_records] Point contact's torque about the normal (Step 1 ('transmits no torque about its normal') vs agreement text ('transmits 0.05–0.16 of the onset torque') and Table 2 point-contact 'arm at spin onset' 0.137/0.068 mm): 0 vs 0.05–0.16 of Drake's onset torque -> The kinetic arm is ~1e-16 mm (twist_slip.jsonl). The 0.068–0.137 mm readings are the onset protocol's offset (deck 23 review #10); report none
- [claims_records] Spinning-arm agreement stated without its rate (Lede and twist paragraph): 'within 1.2 % of the pressure law' -> At 1 rad/s (omega_kin_rad_s). At 1 N the arm is 0.888/0.946/0.963/0.973 mm at 0.1/0.3/1/3 rad/s against the law's 0.974 (deck 23 against-drake)
- [claims_records] 'Creep in the whole task' Future work item was already run (open_list item 3; bed page Future work): 'Re-run the chain's hold and the wield with impratio 1000 ... record the cost per step' -> Backends page 2026-10-06 (chain_impratio.jsonl): the chain completes at impratio 100/1000/3000/10,000 with swing end 90.5/91.4/91.5/91.6° (Drake 91.3°) and physics 73/103/124/148 ms per simulated s. On the deployed turn, raising impratio loses holds (D3 6/9→3/9, D4 8/9→5/9 at 10,000; Drake 9/9, 8/9), with the advice 'keep impratio 100 for manipulation'. Only the wield is untested
- [claims_records] Newton one-world cost (refs_text ('One GPU world costs 0.5 ms per step with reduction and 1.5 ms without') vs Cost section and Table 1 (425 µs)): 0.5 / 1.5 ms -> newton_scaling.jsonl, one world with CUDA graphs: 424.6 µs reduced, 701.8 µs unreduced. The sentence dates from commit c257c5d4 (2026-10-05)
- [claims_records] Newton hydroelastic compared at unmatched friction rows (Agreement paragraph, Figure 7, Table 2 Newton column): Newton hydroelastic at k_f 10 (t_f 3.9 ms): brake swing 130°, turn within 3° on 7/24; pads at t_f = t_c = 10 ms -> Newton tests page: t_f of 10–40 ms removes the overshoot (largest swing 88.7°), and Newton's creep is proportional to t_f, so its 4–7× creep advantage over the pads (6.98 vs 30.76 µm/s) comes from the 3.9 ms setting. The turn replays were never rerun at 10 ms
- [claims_records] The Modulus Future work item (open_list item 5): 'arm scales as E^-1/4; 2–5 MPa lengthens it by 19–50 %' -> Block arm at 1 N: 2.66→2.39 mm over 5→20 MPa (exponent about −0.08; contact sensitivity Table 4). FE pad modulus 12.7 MPa (whole 95A 20 % tip), 14.6 with the magnet, 6.9–16.5 across the print plan, 7.3 from the G-code. Replacement text drafted in docs/handoff/20261007-tpu_tip_finite_elements.md (State 2026-10-09 15:58) was never inserted
- [claims_records] Ratio test for the printed tip (Step 3 last sentence; open_list item 1): 1.565 (foundation) vs 1.817 (Hertz) at 0.5/3 N -> Applies to a spherical tip. On the block the arm grows ×1.08 (pads) and ×1.05 (Drake) from 1 to 3 N. Discriminating bench tests on the FE page: indentation exponent 1.07 vs 1.5 (approach 21.5/41.4/114.9 µm predicted) and twist arm 3.12–3.35 mm vs the pads' 2.72 mm at 3 N

## Reader summaries and gaps

### claims_records: Claims of the topic overview docs/overviews/contact_model.html (builder scripts/contact_overview_page.py, contact_overview_text.py, template) and their consistency with the newer record

The overview's numbers reproduce from the rows its builder reads. Recomputed: μeff 1.005 against Drake's 1.005; spin-onset arm 0.787/0.924/1.193 mm against Drake's 0.839/0.987/1.275 mm at 0.5/1/3 N (−6.2 to −6.5 %); sliding arm +0.5/−1.2/−1.1 % from the integrated law at 1 rad/s; 15.1 against 1,298 µs per CPU step; creep 30.76 against 0.199 µm/s (155×); fixed-Λ̂ stiffness errors 2.83×/0.28× (arm +37/−23 %); GPU 341/313/425 µs for one world and 1.08/1.19/1.85 µs per world-step batched; Figure 7 onset ratio 0.935–0.940, turn within 3° on 23/23/22 of 24, grip 0.98–1.53. Each bed cell is one deterministic run scored against Drake or the pressure-law integral; no row measures a fingertip.

The record narrows what these numbers mean. The bed tip is the 10.55 mm real_v1 sphere. The default tip since 2026-10-13 is the printed TPU block (2.7 mm fillets, 1,059 pads); its only bed rows (2026-10-06 backends page, not linked from the overview) give onset arm +1.2/+4.5 % against Drake, brake force at 45° 0.338 against 0.500 N, creep 11 against 1.2 µm/s (9×), Drake at 9× the pads' CPU cost, and an arm growing as N^0.04–0.07, so the F^(1/4) law, the 1.565/1.817 ratio test and the E^(−1/4) modulus item do not describe it. The bed runs impratio 100 at 1 ms; policies train at impratio 10 and 2 ms (75 µm/s creep on the block). On the servo-limited plant a policy's own actions replayed on another tip hold 1,527 of 1,536 rollouts against 1,531 closed loop. The RL transfer the overview leads with (85/88 against 3/132 replays) is from the 1 N m plant at 44–56 N grips; on the servo plant it is 78/88 against 74/88.

Inside the page the creep factor appears as 100×, 155× and 177× (rows: about 150× on the sphere at 1 ms, 34× at 5 ms). The task-6 sentence (16.8 to 1.0, −0.54 µm/s) is a pooled median. Figure 7 labels sphere-bed columns as the TPU block. Step 5's 205-sphere 45° pad is the chain's, not the bed's 126-sphere 35° pad. Deck 23's corrections (step bound, '2t_c relaxation', body_invweight0, point-contact onset offset, rate-dependent arm) are not applied. The Newton 1.5 ms cost is stale (0.70 ms). The 'creep in the whole task' item already ran on 2026-10-06 and found that raising impratio loses turn holds.

Verdict: Supported, as engineering rather than contact science: spheres sampling a Winkler/hydroelastic pressure law in unmodified MuJoCo, with a closed-form rest stiffness 1/(t_c^2 (1−d0) Λ̂) per sphere, calibrated at load (exact for the solver, mass-independent for 6–98 g tools, 15 regression tests in tests/test_pad_contact_laws.py). On the sphere tip they give hydroelastic friction torque within 4–7 % at onset and about 1 % sliding at 1 rad/s, with identical results in CPU MuJoCo, MuJoCo-Warp and Newton. CPU cost is 86× below Drake on the sphere bed (9× on the block bed). GPU cost per world-step is 1.7–1.9× below Newton hydroelastic (reduced contacts) on the pinch fixture and on the RL state.

Two findings carry beyond this project. Newton's SolverMuJoCo scales hydroelastic stiffness by the effective mass (7.1–7.4× Drake's sink; k_h(w1+w2) restores 1.02–1.04× with no fit). The creep law (with cone saturation) and the step bound in deck 23's corrected form describe MuJoCo bodies with many contacts. 'Better than Newton hydroelastic' depends on the metric: the pads are closer to Drake on torque, slip speed, brake and turn; Newton is closer on creep. Newton was run with its default 3.9 ms friction rows, while the pads got matched rows.

Not supported yet: that the pads reproduce 'a soft fingertip' (FE of the printed tip puts the approach 20–28 % and the arm 7–23 % off; no bench row exists), any bed claim on the default TPU block beyond T1/T2/T5, and a policy-level benefit on the servo-limited plant. For the hand, the pads earn their 1.5–1.7× RL and 3–8× CPU-planner cost where torsional friction decides an open-loop or planned motion: the brake, the open-loop turn and MPC. A standalone paper would need the block bed, the bench torsion, indentation and μ tests, and one task where the pads change a hardware outcome.

### agreement_cost_newton: Agreement with the references (Drake hydroelastic, pressure law), computational cost on CPU and GPU, and fairness of the comparison with Newton's hydroelastic contact

The 1 mm pads agree with Drake better than Newton's hydroelastic tip at every Newton setting the record tested. The hand-level comparison used untuned Newton settings, and Newton's moment-matching reduction never ran in a dynamic task. On the two-pad bed the pads deviate from Drake by a median 6.2 % over nine metrics against 19.3 % for Newton (kf 10 giving 3.87 ms friction rows, reduced contacts, 0.5 mm voxels). Recomputed from the 10-07 rows, Newton at 10 and 20 ms friction rows still gives 18.8 % and 17.9 % over seven metrics. Its reduced contacts reach 0.77-0.88 of the pressure law's arm at 0.25-1 mm voxels, and its braked tool passes 80° at 1.24-1.28 N against 0.39 N for Drake and 0.40 N for the pads. All faces give 1.03-1.04 of the law at 0.5-1 N and fail at 3 N. Newton creeps 3.7-7.0x less than the pads. On the eight-hand turn replay the pads end within 3° of Drake on 23 of 24 placements and hold 40 of 40. Newton ends within 3° on 7 of 24 and holds 33 of 40 (8 and 35 in a second pass). There kf 10 gives about 5 ms friction rows, a value at which the bed's braked tool overshoots past hanging. Newton ran on the hand with 10 ms friction rows only in policy replays at 2 ms and 10 iterations, where it held 49 of 72 servo-plant replays against Drake's 96 of 96.

On one CPU core the pads cost 15.1 µs per 1 ms step on the bed (Drake 1,298.5 µs, 86x), 74.0 µs on the chain (Drake 1 mm mesh 2,407 µs, 33x) and 105.6 µs on the turn replay (Drake 2,082 µs, 20x). That is 3.1-8.2x point contact. Newton has no CPU timing. On the GPU the pads cost 1.08 µs per world-step at 4,096 worlds on the SR2 fixture, against 1.85 µs for Newton reduced and 4.28 µs with all faces. On the D6 RL state they cost 2.41/2.22 µs (MuJoCo-Warp 3.6) or 1.75/1.59 µs (3.14) against Newton's 4.25/4.34 µs at 1,024/2,048 worlds. Newton's own pads cost 4.00 µs, so the margin comes from MuJoCo-Warp's collision path. GPU memory on that state at 1,024 worlds is 4.3-5.7 GB for the pads and 2.6 GB for Newton. In training the pads cost 11-12 % of zero-action env throughput but 1.47-1.82x per PPO iteration, and 11.5-12.5 GB against 2.2-3.3 GB for the TPU mesh. One GPU world (341 µs) is 10x slower than one CPU core (35.2 µs) on the same fixture.

Verdict: Newton fairness verdict. 'Better than Newton hydroelastic' holds against every Newton configuration tested, but two settings aimed at Newton's errors were never tried on the hand: 10 ms friction rows on the 24-placement turn (it ran at about 5 ms) and moment-matching contact reduction (never run dynamically anywhere). Until those runs exist, the margin (6.2 % against 17.9-19.3 % median deviation on the bed, 23 against 7 of 24 on the hand) describes Newton's default pipeline. 'Faster' holds per batched world-step (1.5-2.7x), and the speed comes from MuJoCo-Warp's collision path, since Newton's own pads cost 4.00 µs against its hydroelastic 4.25 µs. It fails on GPU memory on the hand state, where the pads use 1.7-2.2x Newton's memory. 'Fast on CPU' holds against Drake (20-86x), at 3.1-8.2x the cost of point contact; Newton has no CPU number. To settle both claims, run Newton at 10 ms friction rows with moment matching (0.5 and 0.25 mm voxels) on the bed's nine metrics plus the 80° force, and on the 40-placement turn in two GPU passes, scored against Drake with simulator_agreement_figure.py. Time it in the same MuJoCo-Warp build as the pads (newton_scaling.py, same_state_timing.py). Add a 1-, 6- and 12-thread CPU timing and a Newton-on-Warp-CPU timing of the D6 state. The hand-level claim fails if Newton ends within 3° on 20 or more of 24 placements and holds 40 of 40. The bed claim fails if Newton's onset arm reaches 0.95 of the law at 0.5-3 N with an 80° force under 0.5 N. 'Faster' fails if Newton at those settings costs within 1.2x of the pads per world-step.

- Gap: The 24-placement hand turn compared the pads with Newton hydroelastic at kf 10, about 5 ms friction rows on the hand (Eq. 5, w = 42.9 1/kg), with reduced contacts, no moment matching and 0.5 mm voxels. The 10-07 bed settings (10-20 ms friction rows) and moment matching never ran on it, and no all-faces run exists on any hand scene. Plan grips load each finger with 4-16 N, above the 3 N at which all faces failed on the bed. Experiment: Add --tf-ms, --moment-matching, --voxel and --no-reduce to scripts/newton_turn.py: friction gain from rl_policy_replay.match_hydro_friction, HydroelasticSDF.Config(moment_matching=True) at line 169, SDF_VOXEL at line 60. Run D1-D8 x seeds 0-4 with --mass-correct at (a) tf 10 ms, (b) tf 10 ms plus moment matching, (c) (b) at 0.25 mm voxels, (d) tf 10 ms with all faces, (e) (b) with --rl-numerics. Run two passes each, with pad forces logged. Score against plans_drake.jsonl with simulator_agreement_figure.py (within 3°, held, grip ratio). Falsified if any configuration ends within 3° of Drake on at least 20 of 24 pairs and holds 40 of 40 in both passes; the 23 vs 7 of 24 gap is then a Newton setting. Reuse: scripts/newton_turn.py (--mass-correct, --rl-numerics, --contact pads), scripts/rl_policy_replay.py match_hydro_friction, scripts/contact_bed_newton.py NewtonRig._match_friction, scripts/simulator_agreement_figure.py, docs/experiments/20261006-hom_turn3/plans_drake.jsonl Compute: plans_newton_hydro_mc.jsonl stepped one hand (5 worlds, 10.28 s simulated) in 5.7-6.8 s. With SDF build and kernel compile, about 4-6 min per configuration per pass. Five configurations x two passes take about 50 min of GPU, under 2 GB VRAM and one CPU core; check nvidia-smi first. Hardware: False
- Gap: Newton's torque deficit is attributed to contact reduction, but the reduction's moment_matching option never ran in a dynamic task. In Codex's static test it recovered 94 % and 86 % of the unreduced friction envelope at 0.2 and 0.4 mm indentation, against 82 % for the default. Roll and shake never ran at 10 ms friction rows, so no nine-metric score exists for tuned Newton. The early fall in the brake (80° at 1.24-1.28 N against Drake's 0.39 N) is unexplained. Experiment: In scripts/contact_bed_newton.py, pass moment_matching into HydroelasticSDF.Config (line 222) and add presets newton_hydro_mc_tf10_mm and newton_hydro_mc_tf10_mm_vox025. Run static, pull, twist, roll, shake and brake at 0.5, 1 and 3 N with a 1 ms step. Rerun newton_hydro_unreduced_mc static and twist at 3 N with 500 iterations and a sparse Jacobian. During the brake, log the torque of the solver contacts about the pinch axis (NewtonRig.mj_contacts). The claim 'pads better on the bed' is a setting if the onset arm is at least 0.95 of the law at all three loads and the 80° force is under 0.5 N. Reuse: scripts/contact_bed_newton.py (MODELS presets, --outdir, tf presets), scripts/hydroelastic_arm_integral.py, scripts/newton_hydro_tests_page.py, scripts/contact_bed_page.py Compute: One GPU world without a CUDA graph costs about 2.8 ms per step. About 15-20 cases per model at 10-20 s each; three models take 30-45 min on the GPU, under 1 GB VRAM. Hardware: False
- Gap: Pad and Newton costs come from different solver builds: pads on MuJoCo-Warp 3.6 with warp 1.12.1 in mjlab, Newton on MuJoCo-Warp 3.14 with warp 1.17. On the same state the version alone moves the pads from 2.41 to 1.75 µs and the box tip from 1.44 to 2.21 µs per world-step. Newton was timed on the hand only at its cheapest setting (reduced, no moment matching, 0.5 mm voxels). Its all-faces cost (4.28 µs) exists only on the two-finger SR2 fixture. Experiment: Run scripts/gpu_scaling_mjwarp.py and same_state_timing.py --engine mjw inside logs/20261004-contact-transfer/venv, so pads and Newton share MuJoCo-Warp 3.14. Time newton_scaling.py --model hydro_mc --fixture twist and same_state_timing.py --engine nt_hydro with moment matching on, at 0.5 and 0.25 mm voxels and with all faces. Use 1,024-4,096 worlds, two repeats, and a fresh process per point; record GPU MB per world and contacts per world. 'Faster' fails if Newton, at the settings that pass the two experiments above, costs within 1.2x of the pads per world-step. Reuse: scripts/newton_scaling.py (moment_matching=False at line 332), scripts/same_state_timing.py (--buffer-fraction 0.013 --buffer-mult-broad 40), scripts/gpu_scaling_mjwarp.py, logs/20261006-rl_contact/state_* Compute: 10-20 s per batch point; about 40 points take 20-30 min of GPU, with up to 7 GB VRAM at 8,192 worlds with all faces. Hardware: False
- Gap: 'Fast on CPU' rests on single-core numbers against Drake and point contact. The record has no multi-thread throughput on the hand at the trainer's numerics, no condim 4 timing on the hand, and no CPU timing of Newton hydroelastic, although Warp has a CPU device. Experiment: Add an engine 'mjcpu' to scripts/same_state_timing.py that steps the exported D6 state with mujoco.rollout on 1, 6 and 12 threads. Cover the box tip, the TPU mesh at condim 3 and condim 4, and the 2 mm and 1 mm pads, at 2 ms/10 iterations and at 1 ms/100 iterations. Step the same state in Drake on one core (reorient_backends DrakeBench) and in Newton hydroelastic with model.finalize(device='cpu') at 1 and 64 worlds. The CPU claim holds against Newton if Newton on CPU costs more than 10x the pads per step. Reuse: scripts/same_state_timing.py, scripts/rl_state_export.py, scripts/reorient_backends.py (DrakeBench), scripts/hand_object_scale_mpc_timing.py Compute: MuJoCo: 64 worlds x 1,100 steps at 60-110 µs, under 1 min per variant and thread count. Drake: about 2 ms per step, about 3 min for 64 worlds on one core. Newton on CPU is unknown; cap it at 30 min. Run through resguard.sh run --cpu 1300 --mem 4G (12 threads + 1 <= nproc - 4 = 16). Hardware: False
- Gap: Zero-action env steps show the 1 mm pads 11-12 % slower than the box tip. PPO iterations cost 1.47-1.82x the TPU mesh and 11.5-12.5 GB of GPU memory against 2.2-3.3 GB, and the 1 mm pads did not build at 4,096 envs. The share of the extra time spent in collision, contact buffers (640 contacts, 3,072 rows, 768 sensor matches per world), sensors and the solver is unmeasured. Experiment: Profile 20 PPO iterations of rl_train_cube.py --recipe b_liveA at 2,048 envs on the mesh, pads1, pads1 front-half and pads2 scenes from make_pad_morphology_run.py. Put Warp and torch timers around physics, collision, sensors and the PPO update. Repeat pads1 with contact buffers from a calibration pass and at 4,096 envs. A pad variant at or under 1.2x the mesh per iteration, with bed onset torque within 2 % of pads1, replaces pads1 for RL. Reuse: scripts/rl_contact_throughput.py, scripts/make_pad_morphology_run.py, src/morphohand/rl/env_build.py (_contact_buffers), configs/recipes/b_liveA.yaml, scripts/rl_contact_train_queue.sh Compute: About 2-4 min per variant plus the env build; six variants take about 30 min on the GPU with no other GPU job (pads take 11.5 GB). Hardware: False
- Gap: The nine-metric median deviation drops the creep metrics, where Newton creeps 3.7-7.0x less than the pads. It also leaves out the brake's 80° force, where Newton gives 3.3x Drake's value. The published 19.3 % uses Newton at 3.9 ms friction rows, although rows at 10 and 20 ms exist. Experiment: Report per-metric deviations and a control-relevant set (slip force, onset torque at 0.5-3 N, 80° force, largest swing, turn within 3°, held), with creep on its own row. Add Newton's 10 and 20 ms columns from the docs/experiments/20261007-newton_hydro_tests rows. Over seven metrics these give 18.8 % and 17.9 % against the pads' 6.2 %; over force, torque and swing alone, 6.7 % and 6.3 % against 3.7 %. No simulation is needed. Reuse: scripts/contact_bed_page.py, scripts/simulator_agreement_figure.py, docs/experiments/20261007-newton_hydro_tests/*.jsonl Compute: Seconds of Python on existing rows. Hardware: False
- Gap: Every agreement number is relative to Drake, a discretization of the same pressure law at an assumed E of 10 MPa. No measured fingertip torque or creep exists, so which simulator is closer to the printed tip is unknown. Experiment: On the bench, pinch the screwdriver between two printed TPU tips at 0.5, 1 and 3 N on a torque sensor and ramp a twist to spin (bed task 2); then hold half the slip load for 1 s to measure creep. An arm ratio (3 N over 0.5 N) near 1.57 keeps the foundation law that the pads, Drake and Newton's all-faces contact share. An onset torque near 0.8x Drake's would favour Newton's reduced contacts. Reuse: Overview future work 'Hertz or foundation on the printed tip'; scripts/hom_contact_rig.py; scripts/contact_bed_twist.py Compute: One bench session; the matching simulation on hom_contact_rig takes minutes. Hardware: True

### control_evidence: Control and policy level: whether the 1 mm sphere pads change outcomes or decisions against point contact (TPU convex mesh, box tip), condim 4 and Drake

Point contact fails every task that loads the friction torque about the contact normal, and on the servo-limited plant the fingertip model changes no RL outcome. The hand brake, the pick-brake-insert chain and the paper's Exp 2 and Exp 3 fail with point contact in Drake and MuJoCo (one nominal run per model). They complete with 1 mm pads, with condim 4 using a rescheduled torsion coefficient, and with Drake. Condim 4 costs 20.5 ms of physics per simulated second against the pads' 74. On the 1 N m working plant, a grip bonus produced grips of 44–96 N. There, pad and skin finger targets held the tool in 216 of 220 open-loop replays in CPU MuJoCo, Drake and Newton, against 3 of 132 for point contact. Point-contact policies turned the tool further (held cosine 0.84–0.86 against 0.61–0.69). On the servo-limited plant the six final policies hold 1,531 of 1,536 closed-loop rollouts and 1,527 replays of their own actions across four tip models. Replays hold 78 of 88 with pads against 74 of 88 with the mesh, and held cosine is 0.79–0.88 against 0.85–0.90, inside the two-seed spread. The servo makes each fingertip a 250 N/m spring, 139 times softer than the pad contact. The pads keep two advantages there. They hold more rollouts under a weaker servo (kp 0.36: 256 of 256 against 193 of 256), and they hold all CPU-MuJoCo replays (32 of 32 against 23 of 32); the second may be a difference in convex-mesh collision between MuJoCo-Warp and CPU MuJoCo. The owner's stated reason holds at the contact level and on the working plant, and only partly on the realistic plant. No planner has run with a contact model different from its plant: MPC and pinned iCEM roll out the plant's own pad XML, the mu0.7 mismatch hook has no rows, and HOM is kinematic. Point contact picks the same coarse scale-study layout as the pads for 7 of 9 objects, but each object has only 2–3 layouts. In bed task 7 point contact has the same largest stable step as the pads, so a larger step cannot recover the pads' 3.1–7.8 times higher step cost in the planner. The servo-plant page computes the pad brake with the sphere's arm law and underestimates it by a factor of 1.5–2.

Verdict: The pads change control outcomes in two places. The first is tasks that load torsion (brake, braked swing, chain, Exp 2 and Exp 3), where rescheduled condim 4 gives the same result at a third of the cost. The second is transfer between simulators of open-loop plans and of high-grip policies. On the servo-limited plant the pads change neither the RL outcome nor its transfer, apart from a margin under weak actuation. Whether a planner or a policy trainer needs the pads in its own model is untested. The cheapest decisive run is pinned iCEM planning on the point-contact block while the plant runs the pads (about 10 minutes of CPU on six threads).

- Gap: Whether the planner needs the pads in its own rollout model or only in the plant (question i) Experiment: Run pinned iCEM and pinned predictive sampling on the four planner cells × placements 0 and 1 (real_v1, servo plant) in three settings: (a) planner on the point-contact block ('pt' scene), plant on 1 mm pads; (b) planner on pads, plant on 'pt'; (c) both on 'pt'. Code changes: a plan_model key in hand_object_scale_mpc.Planner.__init__ that builds sensor_model from sc['pt'][0] (same nq and nv; sensor_model already raises on a size change), and a --plant-model option in hand_object_scale_planners.py. H1: the pt planner on the pads plant keeps three pads in at least 7 of 8 runs, with a median held turn within 15° of the pads planner's 65°. A median below 50° or lost three-pad contact falsifies H1 and means the plans rely on pad torsion that the pt model lacks. ### bench_sim2real: Hardware data for validating the fingertip contact model against the bench, and the printed-tip calibration

No measurement of the fingertip contact exists. The only tracked hardware data are 177 complete open-loop reorientation runs of the eight deployed hands D1–D8, recorded 2026-08-31 to 09-02 (docs/experiments/20260902-cb1-log-archive/logs, 251 runs in all). The fingers carried the printed TPU insert (17 × 14.8 × 22 mm, about 2.7 mm fillets, print settings unrecorded). The tool was a printed 25 × 100 mm cylinder with a 40 mm tag vane whose centre sits 77 mm from the cylinder centre. The vaned mass was never weighed, and the simulator models the bare 24.5 g cylinder.

Each run logs 55 command frames at 50 Hz over the 1.08 s turn. Servo positions exist at three instants only (−2.57, −0.01 and +3.00 s), so no joint data cover the turn. The CB1 log holds the tag's tilt, height and x/y at about 8 Hz; the 30 Hz traces exist only in the gitignored logs/tracker. Six of the 177 runs carry servo_load. Tag noise is 0.017° static and 0.05–0.09° during a hold. Under identical commands, the end turn of held runs varies 3.4–23.6° within a hand (pooled SD 12.9°).

Only the 1 mm pads have been replayed on these commands (7,979 replays over 288 plants, scripts/bench_replay_calibration.py). On the working plant they match the joint readbacks to 2.89° but the tool turn only to a median 14.7°. The correlation with the bench turn is r 0.47, the per-hand offsets run from −22.9 to +15.8°, and the simulation drops the tool in 0 of 177 runs against 36 on the bench. The readbacks identify neither the servo gain (0.25–30 N m/rad all within 2.53–2.75°) nor μ between 0.6 and 1.0. Lowering μ to 0.6 cuts the simulated turn by 10°, and the choice of servo plant moves it between 11 and 39°.

In plan replays the pads sit a median 1.3° from Drake and point contact 3.3°; the exception is D7, where point contact turns 31° further. On six hands the differences between contact models are therefore smaller than the bench's standard error (1.1–4.5°) and 3–20 times smaller than the offsets. The D7 bench rest also involves contact with the palm and servo bank, which the replay scenes model at the old plate height (25 mm too close to the fingertips) with no geometry for the servo bank. A bench-replay check of the contact models costs under an hour of CPU (pads 138 s, Drake about 35 min for 177 runs) and has almost no power to separate them.

Validating the contact model needs fixture tests on a printed tip (μ, twist at 1 and 3 N, indentation, presliding), the servo push test, and an in-hand pinch brake that separates point from distributed contact. The finite-element calibration supplies the predictions:
- pad modulus 13.0 MPa for the 95A 20 % gyroid tip, and 7.3 MPa for the same tip modelled as printed from its G-code;
- twist arm at 3 N of 3.12 mm (finite elements) against 2.72 mm (pads);
- presliding of 25 µm at 1 N.

Verdict: A bench-replay check of the contact models on the existing 177 runs is feasible but tells little. The tooling exists and runs in under an hour of CPU. The bench's within-hand scatter (12.9°) and its systematic offsets (up to 23°) exceed the pads-to-Drake differences (at most 3.1°) and the point-to-pads differences on 6 of 8 hands (at most 4.4°). Only D7 can test point contact. There the pads match the 08-31 bench to 1.6° (n 10, SD 3.4°) and point contact turns 31° further in plan replays. That test is valid only if a replay with the built plate height (G2) shows the palm contact does not set D7's rest.

Bench time should go first to μ (pull test, 30 min) and servo stiffness (push test, 39 min), because those two change policy outcomes. Swapping the tip model changed the policies' final tool angle by at most 0.054 in cosine with vertical. Next come the twist, indentation and presliding tests on a fixture (about 1.5 h), which validate the pads on the real tip. An in-hand pinch brake is the one test in the hand whose contact-model differences exceed the bench scatter.

Compute μ = F/(2N) and run tpu_tip_calibrate.py fit --pull.

Decision rules:
- μ below 0.55 lies outside the RL friction randomisation (×0.55–1.15 of 1.0); retrain with a wider range.

### implementation: Sphere-pad implementation (builders, calibration, MuJoCo/MuJoCo-Warp/mjlab/Newton paths), tests, packaging, and harnesses for experiments (a)-(e)

The production pads are built by reorient_backends.replace_tips on fingertip_geometry.pad_spheres. The printed TPU block (17.0 x 14.8 x 22.0 mm, 2.7 mm fillets) is sampled at 1 mm on every face except the back and top, giving 1,059 spheres of 0.75 mm radius per tip and 3,177 per hand. The sphere centres are inset by the radius. Each pad uses condim 3, friction (mu,0,0), priority 1, mass 0, margin 0, solref (10 ms, 1) and solimp (d0,d0,...). Each finger gets one d0 = 1 - 1/(tc^2 K Lambda), using the median pad stiffness K = E a/h = 1,200 N/m (E 10 MPa, h 8.5 mm) and Lambda = body_invweight0[tip] + body_invweight0[tool] from a compile of the scene. This gives d0 = 0.806 on the welded bench palm, 0.811 in the RL scene and 0.892 on the jointed rig.

An older builder (hom_contact_rig, hom_chain) puts Fibonacci caps of 126 spheres (35 deg) or 205 spheres (45 deg) on the 10.55 mm sphere tip with h = R. The overview's equations, the bed's Table 2 and its 15 us cost refer to this builder, not to the block pads.

The 15 contact-law tests pass on MuJoCo 3.6 in 15.6 s. The calibration is exact only under four conditions:
- the elliptic cone (a one-sphere probe gives 12,000 and 120,000 N/m under the pyramidal cone at impratio 10 and 100);
- dt <= 5 ms, because the refsafe clamp softens K by (tc/2dt)^2 above that;
- diagexact off (MuJoCo >= 3.9);
- contact with the one calibrated partner. Pads that touch a static geom are 8.9-19.7x too stiff, which is why the RL scenes restrict pads to the tool (6,464 allowed pairs, against 3.4 million in the bench and scale-study scenes).

MuJoCo-Warp reads weld-body inverse weights, which changes K by +0.06 % on the hand. mjlab's mass, armature or body_pos randomisation recomputes body_invweight0 and would detune every pad. Newton needs three settings: d0 from solver.mj_model, kf matched to 10 ms, and kh x Lambda for hydroelastic contact. Its turn rows ran with 5.2 ms friction rows and contact reduction, so the comparison of 23 against 7 of 24 placements was not made at Newton's best settings.

Cost on the hand:
- CPU: 105-131 us per 1 ms step, 8-9x the TPU-mesh point contact and 24x cheaper than Drake; 31 us when nothing touches.
- GPU: 2.2-2.7 us per world-step at 2,048 worlds, 1.5-2.3x the mesh.
- RL training: 1.74x per PPO iteration and 11.5 GB of GPU memory.

All five experiments can run on the existing harnesses with 5-40 lines of new code each and about 3 h of compute in total. None needs hardware.

Verdict: The implementation is a recipe on unmodified MuJoCo: surface sampling into inset spheres, a one-line per-finger impedance calibration from compiled inverse weights, and collision filtering to the tool. It is correct within its tested envelope (15/15 tests on 3.6, exact static law on the rig) and carries to MuJoCo-Warp and Newton with two documented corrections. Five assumptions have no guard in code: elliptic cone, dt <= tc/2, diagexact off, a single partner body, and no runtime recompute of invweight0. The record does not yet show the claimed speed advantage at the hand level on CPU (8-9x point contact). On GPU the pads cost 1.5-2.3x the mesh per physics step, 1.74x per PPO iteration and 5x the memory. As a standalone library it is 2-3 days of work, mostly generalising the hand-specific sampler and adding guards. The library content is modest (calibration plus guards); a section or appendix suits it better than a standalone paper. The experiments that matter for throughput are (a) step size, (b) planning on a cheaper model and (c, d) the cost split and sphere count. Experiment (e) is needed before any claim against Newton hydroelastic.

The planner always rolls out the plant's own pad model, except for the GPU planners: float32, 20 iterations, pads filtered to the cylinder. Experiment: Three mismatches, each scored with the scale-turn page's rule: a planner transfers if three pads stay loaded in >= 7 of 8 runs and its median held turn falls by < 15 deg against the matched planner (icem_pin 64.6, ps_pin 42.3 deg).
